      <span class="pill">The 3-day AI RevOps operating system</span>
      <h1 style="margin-top:16px">Build a Funnel That Turns Strangers Into Retained Customers — In 3 Days.</h1>
      <p class="sub">This is the exact system we use to take someone who's never heard of you and walk them all the way to <strong>money in the bank — and then keep them paying month after month.</strong></p>
      <p class="lead" style="margin-top:20px">Here's the deal: most people build funnels backwards. They obsess over the logo, the colors, the "perfect" landing page — and never stop to ask the only question that matters: <em>what has to be true for a stranger to pay me?</em></p>
      <p>Over the next 3 days, you'll answer that question with a real, working funnel. Not theory. Not a course you'll never finish. An actual engine — pages, templates, automations, and an AI orchestrator — that runs while you sleep.</p>
      <div class="box do"><div class="h">◆ What you'll walk away with</div>
        <p>A complete AI RevOps engine: a strategy on one page, a conversion engine that feeds every asset, all your funnel pages and templates, an AI orchestrator running the operations, and the unit-economics to scale it to $1M ARR.</p></div>

<section style="--hue:#6366F1;--hue-ink:color-mix(in srgb,#6366F1 70%,var(--ink))" id="revops">
      <div class="chap"><span class="no">Foundations</span><span class="tag">The Thing You're Actually Building</span></div>
      <h2>What Is AI RevOps?</h2>
      <p class="lead">RevOps is one word for something most companies run as two departments — and the gap between those two departments is where most revenue quietly dies.</p>
      <p><strong>Marketing owns the top.</strong> The ads, the content, the posts, the emails you send, the messages you send, the billboard, the spot on TV. <strong>Sales owns the bottom.</strong> The call, the demo, the proposal, the close. Between them sits a handoff: marketing counts leads, sales counts deals, and nobody owns the distance in between. Ask both teams why a quarter missed and you'll get two confident answers that don't touch each other.</p>
      <p><strong>Revenue operations is the decision to stop running it as two jobs.</strong> One system, one owner, one set of numbers — from the first second a total stranger sees your name, to the moment money clears your bank account.</p>
      <p>Trace the whole thing once and it stops feeling abstract. Someone who has never heard of you sees something: an ad in a feed, a founder's post, a cold email, a message, a billboard on a commute, thirty seconds on TV. That touch has exactly one job — be seen by the right person. They click. They land somewhere. They hand over an email or a number, or they don't. They get a sequence. They turn up to something: a page, a session, a call. They're qualified, or they aren't. They see an offer. They buy. The payment settles. They get onboarded — and then they either stay or they leave.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Cold stranger to cleared payment — one system, not two departments</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 528" role="img" aria-label="The revenue operations system. Across the top, a seven-stage journey from stranger through attention, lead, nurtured, qualified and buyer to cleared payment. Brackets below show which stages marketing traditionally owns and which sales owns, with the handoff between them marked as the point where revenue leaks. Beneath, four infrastructure layers — touchpoints, people, data and plumbing — with an AI orchestrator column reading and writing to all four.">

        <div class="cap"><b>Read the top row as a set of numbers, not a story.</b> Seven stages, seven counts, six rates between them. The moment you own all of them you stop arguing about whose fault a bad quarter was.</div>
      </div>
      <p>Here's the part that's actually new. Those four layers — touchpoints, people, data, plumbing — have always existed. Every company has had them, in some state, forever. What changed is that all four are now <strong>addressable by software that can reason.</strong> An agent can read the CRM, write the sequence, watch the step rates, spot which one dropped, draft the fix and schedule the send. Not because the model is magic, but because each layer already exposes an API and something can finally hold all of them at once.</p>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ So what does the "AI" in AI RevOps actually mean</div>
        <p>Not a chatbot bolted onto a website. It means <strong>the operator of the system is an agent rather than a person with twelve browser tabs open.</strong> You still decide the strategy, the offer and what ships. The agent does the reading, the writing, the wiring and the measuring — the work that was previously split across a marketer, an ops person and a sales manager who each saw a third of the picture.</p></div>
      <div class="box do"><div class="h">◆ Where you'll be by Friday</div>
        <p>You'll have the knowledge, the tools, the prompts, the workflows and the automations to stand this entire system up on your own — cold stranger to cleared payment, instrumented end to end, with an agent operating it.</p></div>

<section style="--hue:#3B82F6;--hue-ink:color-mix(in srgb,#3B82F6 70%,var(--ink))" id="whyfunnel">
      <div class="chap"><span class="no">Foundations</span><span class="tag">Why This Shape, and Not Just a Better Product</span></div>
      <h2>Why Do You Need a Funnel — A Conversion Engine?</h2>
      <p class="lead">Here's the deal: people <strong>buy on a promise</strong> and <strong>stay on a result.</strong> Those are two different forces, and almost every funnel ever built addresses only the first one.</p>
      <p>You can be genuinely excellent at earning the yes and still lose the customer — because nothing you built does anything about the gap between the moment they pay and the moment the thing actually works for them. That gap is where churn lives. It is also, almost always, invisible on the dashboard that told you the campaign was a success.</p>
      <p>So a conversion engine is not a machine for getting a yes. <strong>It's a machine for delivering value continuously</strong> — before the sale, to earn the yes, and after it, to make the yes hold.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Excitement peaks at purchase. Value arrives later. The gap is the churn window.</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 38 860 414" role="img" aria-label="A chart over ninety days from purchase. One curve shows excitement peaking at the moment of purchase then decaying. A second curve shows experienced value starting at zero and rising slowly, crossing the excitement curve late; the shaded area before that crossing is labelled the churn window. A third dashed curve shows value rising far faster when an on-ramp is in place, crossing early and leaving a much narrower window.">

          <line x1="16" y1="372" x2="844" y2="372" class="gridline"/>
          <text x="16" y="394" class="svlabel" font-size="11.5">THE ENGINE'S REAL JOB</text>
          <text x="16" y="414" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">Not to raise the excitement curve — that peaks on its own and decays no matter what you do. It is to drag the VALUE curve left, until it</text>
          <text x="16" y="432" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">crosses before the excitement runs out. Everything you build this week is, underneath, an attempt to move that crossing point earlier.</text>

      <h3 id="whyfunnel-why-the-sale-alone-doesn-t-hold-it">Why the Sale Alone Doesn't Hold It</h3>
      <p>Software is supposed to be stickier than a course. It isn't — not on its own. Russell Brunson puts the mechanism precisely, crediting it to Bill Glazer: what he calls the <em>pain of disconnect</em> is not a property of the software at all. It's a property of <strong>what the customer has built inside it.</strong> Someone who never deployed anything churns exactly like a course they never opened. What creates the switching cost is usage — and what creates usage is being taught.</p>
      <p>Alex Hormozi's SaaS case study is the cleanest version of this. His team replaced a 25-person group onboarding call with a one-to-one session that restated the customer's own goal, delivered a single quick win, and booked the next meeting from inside that meeting so nobody was left in no-man's-land. Ascension to the next offer went from 20% to 48%. His principle is worth writing down: <em>the best way to get someone to expect value in the future is to give them value today.</em></p>
      <p>Dan Martell's version is blunter. His own company ran 12% monthly churn — a nine-month customer. A client of his went from 18% to 4% monthly by building an on-ramp (teach the movements before the first class), a peer community so people feel part of something rather than merely using a tool, visible case studies, and a member-at-risk monitor.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Four different mechanisms — they need four different assets</div>

          <text x="16" y="284" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">These are four separate jobs. A library of tutorials only does the first and third — which is why tutorial-only retention strategies plateau.</text>
        </svg>
      </div>
      <h3 id="whyfunnel-why-every-brand-you-can-name-already-does-this">Why Every Brand You Can Name Already Does This</h3>
      <p>Look at any well-known software or service brand and you will find the same set of things, without exception. They <strong>publish content continuously.</strong> They <strong>run webinars and live sessions.</strong> They <strong>write books.</strong> They <strong>run workshops and events.</strong> They <strong>host a community.</strong></p>
      <p>None of that is charity and none of it is "brand awareness." Every one of those is a <strong>carefully built conversion engine</strong> — a mechanism that takes someone cold, gives them something genuinely useful, and moves them one step closer to buying. And then keeps running after they've bought, because staying on top of mind is what turns a customer into a member of a tribe. The same asset does two jobs depending on which side of the sale the reader is standing on.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">The same five assets, working both sides of the sale</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 383" role="img" aria-label="Five assets — content, live sessions, a book, workshops and events, and community — each shown with the job it does before the sale on the left and the job it does after the sale on the right, arranged around a central spine labelled the same asset.">
          <defs>

            <text x="340" y="194" text-anchor="end" class="svlabel" font-size="10.5">Buys authority you can't claim</text><text x="340" y="210" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">and filters for serious readers</text>
            <text x="520" y="194" class="svlabel" font-size="10.5">Gives the method a name</text><text x="520" y="210" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">something to belong to</text>
            <rect x="380" y="238" width="100" height="52" rx="10" fill="var(--accent)"/><text x="430" y="260" text-anchor="middle" class="svwhite" font-size="10.5">Workshops</text><text x="430" y="276" text-anchor="middle" class="svwhite2" font-size="9">&amp; events</text>
            <text x="340" y="258" text-anchor="end" class="svlabel" font-size="10.5">Lets them feel it working</text><text x="340" y="274" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">experience beats description</text>

            <text x="340" y="322" text-anchor="end" class="svlabel" font-size="10.5">Proof that people like them win</text><text x="340" y="338" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">peers are more credible than you</text>
            <text x="520" y="322" class="svlabel" font-size="10.5">The reason they stay past the result</text><text x="520" y="338" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">identity, not utility</text>
          </g>
          <g fill="none" stroke="var(--accent)" stroke-width="1.5" opacity=".55" marker-end="url(#wfL)">
            <path d="M376 72 H348" class="flowline"/><path d="M376 136 H348" class="flowline"/><path d="M376 200 H348" class="flowline"/><path d="M376 264 H348" class="flowline"/><path d="M376 328 H348" class="flowline"/>

          <g fill="none" stroke="var(--teal)" stroke-width="1.5" opacity=".65" marker-end="url(#wfR)">
            <path d="M484 72 H512" class="flowline"/><path d="M484 136 H512" class="flowline"/><path d="M484 200 H512" class="flowline"/><path d="M484 264 H512" class="flowline"/><path d="M484 328 H512" class="flowline"/>
          </g>
        </svg>
        <div class="cap"><b>You are not building five things.</b> You're building five wrappers around one engine — and each one keeps earning after the sale, which is the half almost everybody skips.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="srcnote"><b>On the numbers people quote here.</b> A Forrester Consulting study commissioned by Intellum (2024, 300 director-level-and-above decision-makers) reported roughly <b>22% higher product retention</b>, <b>38.3% higher adoption</b> and <b>35% higher lifetime value per trainee</b>. Treat those as directional, not proof: the study was commissioned by a customer-education vendor and the figures are self-reported by the people who run those programmes. A TSIA survey of about 2,800 customers found <b>68% say they use the product more after training</b> — that is perceived behaviour, not measured usage. TSIA itself notes only <b>11%</b> of firms have ever analysed whether content consumption actually affects renewal. Use them to argue direction; never as a promised result.</div>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ The honest limit — read this before you over-build tutorials</div>
        <p><strong>Results alone plateau.</strong> Brunson's own position in <em>Expert Secrets</em> is that if the only thing you ever do is help someone achieve a result, they will eventually leave you anyway. Enablement gets them to the result. <strong>Community and a visible next rung are what keep them past it.</strong> Build both, or you'll do all this work and still watch people go quiet at month nine.</p></div>
      <div class="box do"><div class="h">◆ What this means for the next three days</div>
        <p>Every page, sequence and automation you build is in service of one thing: <strong>moving the value crossing earlier</strong> and then continuing to deliver value on the far side of the sale. If a thing you're about to build does neither, it's decoration — cut it.</p></div>
    </section>
<div class="divider"></div>
<section style="--hue:#06B6D4;--hue-ink:color-mix(in srgb,#06B6D4 70%,var(--ink))" id="funnels">
  <div class="chap"><span class="no">Foundations</span><span class="tag">Three Shapes, Every Stage, Every Tool</span></div>
  <h2>What Are Funnels?</h2>
  <p class="lead">A funnel is the path a stranger walks to become a paying customer — and then a returning one. Every funnel has the same jobs: get attention, capture it, keep it warm, convert it, get paid, and keep them. What changes is the <strong>shape</strong>: the moment and the format in which you ask.</p>
  <p>These are the three shapes this programme builds in depth, each played as the same machine you saw on the masterclass page: every stage of the Tantra Pro Funnel and Tantra Pro Flow, the tools that run it and the maths between stages, then the AI engine and the finished system. <strong>Scroll inside each frame to play it.</strong></p>
  <div class="fo" data-funnel="webinar">
  <div class="fo-top"><div><div class="fo-k">Funnel 1 of 3 · 6 stages</div><h4 id="funnels-the-webinar-funnel">The Webinar Funnel</h4></div>
    <a class="fo-open" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Open full screen ↗</a></div>
  <p>Invite named people to one dated live session. Teach the mechanism, then make the offer once, live, near the end.</p>
  <div class="fo-pills"><span><b>Use it when</b> Your buyer is list-addressable — you can build a searchable list of them and invite them by name.</span><span><b>Mechanism</b> One live session → booked calls</span></div>
  <div class="fo-frame"><iframe title="The webinar funnel, animated"></iframe><span class="fo-hint">Scroll inside to play it stage by stage ↓</span></div>
</div>
  <div class="fo" data-funnel="rsp">
  <div class="fo-top"><div><div class="fo-k">Funnel 2 of 3 · 5 stages</div><h4 id="funnels-the-reverse-squeeze-page-funnel">The Reverse Squeeze Page Funnel</h4></div>
    <a class="fo-open" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Open full screen ↗</a></div>
  <p>Lead with value to strangers — a VSL on the page, no opt-in wall — and only then ask. The page squeezes after it gives.</p>
  <div class="fo-pills"><span><b>Use it when</b> Your buyer is content-reachable — they already gather somewhere you can put value in front of them.</span><span><b>Mechanism</b> VSL landing page → qualified booked calls</span></div>
  <div class="fo-frame"><iframe title="The reverse squeeze page funnel, animated"></iframe><span class="fo-hint">Scroll inside to play it stage by stage ↓</span></div>
</div>
  <div class="fo" data-funnel="challenge">
  <div class="fo-top"><div><div class="fo-k">Funnel 3 of 3 · 6 stages</div><h4 id="funnels-the-challenge-funnel">The Challenge Funnel</h4></div>
    <a class="fo-open" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Open full screen ↗</a></div>
  <p>A short live cohort — here, three days — where people build alongside you each day. Commitment grows day by day, and the offer lands on someone who has already done the work.</p>
  <div class="fo-pills"><span><b>Use it when</b> Your buyer can show up three days running — a cohort needs repeated attention.</span><span><b>Mechanism</b> 3 live days → build → upgrade</span></div>
  <div class="fo-frame"><iframe title="The challenge funnel, animated"></iframe><span class="fo-hint">Scroll inside to play it stage by stage ↓</span></div>
</div>

<section style="--hue:#10B981;--hue-ink:color-mix(in srgb,#10B981 70%,var(--ink))" id="bigpic">
      <div class="chap"><span class="no">Foundations</span><span class="tag">Read This First</span></div>
      <h2>The Big Picture: One System, Three Shapes</h2>
      <p class="lead">Strip away the jargon and every funnel on earth — a live workshop, a quiz, a video sales letter, an Amazon product page — does the same four things in the same order. What changes is the <em>shape</em>, not the system.</p>
      <h3 id="bigpic-the-daily-rhythm-watch-fill-get-do">The Daily Rhythm: Watch → Fill → Get → Do</h3>
      <p>Every day of the workshop runs the same four beats.</p>
      <div class="grid g2">
        <div class="card"><div class="n">1 · Watch</div><b>The Live Session</b><span>Teaching plus Q&amp;A, live. No recording-only days — you're building alongside everyone else.</span></div>
        <div class="card"><div class="n">2 · Fill</div><b>The Survey, in Parallel</b><span>A full-screen form open next to the session. Every question has sample answers so you always know what "good" looks like.</span></div>
        <div class="card"><div class="n">3 · Get</div><b>Your Generated Output</b><span>Your answers run through an AI workflow and come back as a PDF plus a live link — by WhatsApp and email, same day.</span></div>
        <div class="card"><div class="n">4 · Do</div><b>The Short Homework</b><span>One small task that unblocks tomorrow. Skipping it is the single biggest reason people fall behind.</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="srcnote"><b>A note on the numbers.</b> The figures in the diagrams are <b>working teaching models</b>, not client results. They come from a reference funnel spec and exist to show you the <em>shape</em> of a healthy funnel and the arithmetic that connects the stages. Your numbers will differ. The whole point of Day 3 is that you fetch your own.</div>
      <h3 id="bigpic-every-funnel-has-4-jobs">Every Funnel Has 4 Jobs</h3>
      <p>Traffic, capture, convert, retain. That's it. Every tool, page and automation you will build this week exists to do one of those four things — and the reason funnels beat "post and pray" is that you can see which of the four is broken.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">

      <h3 id="bigpic-the-whole-machine-on-one-page">The Whole Machine, on One Page</h3>
      <p>Those four jobs get delivered by <strong>six stages</strong>. This is the master map — the thing you'll be building for the rest of the week. Print it. Everything in Days 1–5 is a zoom-in on one of these bands.</p>

        <div class="cap"><b>Read it top to bottom once, then bottom to top.</b> Top-down is how a stranger travels. Bottom-up is how the money travels: S6 keeps them, S5 books them, S3 shows them up, S2 captures them, S1 finds them. <b>Figures are teaching models, not client results.</b></div>
      </div>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ Why six stages and not four</div>
        <p>The four jobs are the <strong>theory</strong>. The six stages are the <strong>build</strong>. "Capture" sounds like one thing until you realise a landing page (S2) and a show-up sequence (S3) are entirely different assets with entirely different failure modes — and that a funnel with a great page and no nurture loses more than half its registrations before the event even starts.</p></div>
      <h3 id="bigpic-one-funnel-three-shapes">One Funnel, Three Shapes</h3>
      <p>You're not all building the same funnel. Some of you need a live webinar. Some need a multi-day workshop. Some need a reverse squeeze page that gives value before it asks for anything. <strong>Same six stages. Different shape at each one.</strong></p>
      <div class="viz wide">

      <div class="fvar">
        <div class="fh">◆ And the other 17 types?</div>
        <div class="fb">
          <div class="fc w"><b>They're Recombinations</b><span>A product launch is a webinar funnel stretched over four videos. A book funnel is a reverse squeeze where Part 1 is 200 pages. A challenge is a webinar that refuses to end.</span></div>
          <div class="fc k"><b>Same Six Stages</b><span>Every one of the 20 types in the library maps onto S1–S6. What differs is which stage carries the weight — and how many hours it costs you to build.</span></div>
          <div class="fc r"><b>You Pick Yours on Day 1</b><span>All 20 are laid out grouped by the job they do, with a build-hour estimate attached to each — so the choice is made against real cost, not vibes.</span></div>
        </div>
      </div>
      <h3 id="bigpic-where-your-offers-live-the-value-ladder">Where Your Offers Live: The Value Ladder</h3>
      <p>Before you pick a funnel type, you need to know <strong>what the funnel is selling into.</strong> Almost nobody buys the expensive thing first. They buy a small thing, it works, and they climb.</p>
      <p>Here's the deal: a funnel isn't a machine for selling one product. It's a machine for <em>moving someone up a ladder.</em> And the ladder has a shape — each rung asks for more commitment and returns more value, while the crowd on each rung gets smaller.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">The value ladder — commitment rises, audience narrows, value compounds</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 486" role="img" aria-label="Value ladder staircase: five ascending rungs plotted with commitment on the horizontal axis and value delivered and trust earned on the vertical axis. Rungs are attention, lead, entry offer, core program and flagship. A narrowing wedge behind the stairs shows the audience shrinking at each rung, and a dashed ascension path climbs the steps.">
          <defs>
            <marker id="va" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path d="M1 1L8 5L1 9" fill="none" stroke="var(--teal)" stroke-width="1.8" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"/></marker>
          </defs>
          <line x1="90" y1="438" x2="838" y2="438" class="axis"/>
          <line x1="90" y1="42" x2="90" y2="438" class="axis"/>
          <text x="464" y="470" text-anchor="middle" class="svsub" font-size="11.5">COMMITMENT  →  attention · contact details · money · more money</text>
          <text transform="translate(28,240) rotate(-90)" text-anchor="middle" class="svsub" font-size="11.5">VALUE DELIVERED · TRUST EARNED  →</text>
          <g>

            <text x="163" y="392" class="svwhite" font-size="12">Attention</text><text x="163" y="409" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">free content</text><text x="163" y="425" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">costs them nothing</text>
            <text x="309" y="324" class="svwhite" font-size="12">Lead</text><text x="309" y="341" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">opt-in / registration</text><text x="309" y="357" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">costs them an email</text>
            <text x="455" y="256" class="svwhite" font-size="12">Entry offer</text><text x="455" y="273" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">small, self-liquidating</text><text x="455" y="289" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">pays for your traffic</text>
            <text x="601" y="188" class="svwhite" font-size="12">Core program</text><text x="601" y="205" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">the transformation</text><text x="601" y="221" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">course · community · calls</text>
            <text x="747" y="120" class="svwhite" font-size="12">Flagship</text><text x="747" y="137" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">done-with-you, long horizon</text><text x="747" y="153" class="svwhite2" font-size="10">your highest-trust buyers</text>
          </g>
          <path d="M120 356 H206 V288 H352 V220 H498 V152 H644 V84 H790" fill="none" stroke="var(--teal)" stroke-width="2.4" stroke-dasharray="6 6" class="flowline" marker-end="url(#va)" opacity=".95"/>
          <text x="90" y="20" class="svsub" font-size="10.5">HOW MANY PEOPLE STAND ON EACH RUNG</text>
          <g text-anchor="middle" font-size="10">
            <rect x="98" y="30" width="130" height="9" rx="4.5" fill="var(--accent)" opacity=".85"/>
            <text x="163" y="52" class="svmute">everyone who arrives</text>
            <rect x="273" y="30" width="72" height="9" rx="4.5" fill="var(--accent)" opacity=".7"/>
            <text x="309" y="52" class="svmute">a fraction opt in</text>
            <rect x="435" y="30" width="40" height="9" rx="4.5" fill="var(--accent)" opacity=".55"/>
            <text x="455" y="52" class="svmute">a fraction of those buy</text>
            <rect x="590" y="30" width="22" height="9" rx="4.5" fill="var(--accent)" opacity=".42"/>
            <text x="601" y="52" class="svmute">some buyers climb</text>
            <rect x="741" y="30" width="12" height="9" rx="4.5" fill="var(--teal)" opacity=".8"/>
            <text x="747" y="52" class="svmute">a handful reach the top</text>
          </g>
          <line x1="90" y1="60" x2="820" y2="60" stroke="var(--line-2)" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3 4"/>
        </svg>
        <div class="cap"><b>The load-bearing rung is the entry offer.</b> Price it so the revenue it produces covers the cost of the traffic that produced it, and your top of funnel becomes free — every rung above it is then pure margin.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="box eg"><div class="h">◆ Example · this workshop's own ladder</div>
        <p>You're standing on one right now. Free content brought you here. The 3-Day AI RevOps Workshop is the entry offer — deliberately priced to cover the cost of filling it. Inside it you get a month of the app included. Above it sits a core program (course, community, group coaching) and above that a flagship tier (long-horizon access plus one-to-one implementation). <strong>Five rungs, one system, and each rung sells the next by working.</strong></p></div>
      <div class="box tip"><div class="h">◆ Pro tip · build the ladder before the funnel</div>
        <p>If you only have one thing to sell, your funnel has to do all the persuading in one shot — and your traffic has to be profitable on the very first transaction. That's the hardest game in marketing. <strong>Two rungs is dramatically easier than one.</strong> If you don't have a second rung yet, write one down today; you'll need it by Day 3.</p></div>
      <p>Most businesses are great at one of these and terrible at the rest. A "viral" post with no capture step leaks every visitor. A beautiful sales page with no traffic is a billboard in the desert. <strong>The funnel's job is to make all four work together.</strong></p>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ Why retention is the whole game</div>
        <p>Acquiring a customer costs money. Keeping one costs almost nothing. That's why the north star of this entire system isn't the first sale — it's <strong>MRR: recurring revenue that compounds month after month.</strong> Everything before it exists to earn Day 3's retention.</p></div>
      <h3 id="bigpic-the-one-equation-to-memorize">The One Equation to Memorize</h3>
      <p>You don't need an MBA. You need this:</p>
      <div class="box eg"><div class="h">The funnel equation</div>
        <p style="font-size:19px;font-weight:600;color:var(--ink)">Profit = (Traffic × Conversion rate × Price) − Cost to deliver</p>
        <p style="margin-top:10px">Pull any one lever up and profit moves. The magic is that <strong>you can pull all four</strong> — and a funnel is just the machine that lets you pull them on purpose instead of by accident.</p></div>
      <p>That's the version you can hold in your head. But it has a flaw: <em>"conversion rate" is a guess.</em> On Day 1 you'll replace every symbol in that equation with a number your funnel actually reports — a counted event with a named source — and by Day 3 you'll be reading those numbers weekly and fixing whichever one is lowest.</p>
      <div class="box deep"><div class="h">◆ The rule that runs through the whole week</div>
        <p><strong>Never model your funnel with invented rates.</strong> Every step you build gets instrumented as an event. The count of that event is the metric. A "conversion rate" is just one count divided by another. If you can't fetch it, you can't fix it — and you'll be optimising by vibes.</p></div>

Skipping the homework is the single biggest reason people fall behind, because each day's session assumes yesterday's output exists.</p></div>

25:T13b40,<section style="--hue:#F59E0B;--hue-ink:color-mix(in srgb,#F59E0B 70%,var(--ink))" id="d1">
      <div class="chap"><span class="no">Day 1</span><span class="tag">Strategize · The Offer, the Audience and the Engine</span></div>
      <h2>Decide What You Sell, and Exactly Who You Sell It To</h2>
      <p class="lead">Day 1 uses zero tools and decides 90% of the outcome. Everything you build on Days 2 and 3 is generated <em>from</em> today's answers — so a vague answer today becomes a vague landing page on Wednesday and a dead campaign next month.</p>
      <h3 id="d1-funnel-vs-random-marketing">Funnel vs. Random Marketing</h3>
      <p>Random marketing is post-and-pray. You publish, you boost, you hope. A funnel is different: <strong>every step has one job, and each step earns the next.</strong> If a visitor doesn't take the next action, that step is broken — and you know exactly which one.</p>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ The mindset shift</div>
        <p>If a customer doesn't understand or use what you sell, that's <strong>not their fault — it's yours.</strong> Your funnel owns the whole journey, from "who are you?" to "I can't run my business without this." Own the whole journey and retention stops being a department.</p></div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-funnel-vs-random-marketing"></div>
<h3 id="d1-the-three-questions-every-funnel-answers">The Three Questions Every Funnel Answers</h3>
      <ol>
        <li><strong>Who is this for?</strong> Specific enough that the right person reads it and thinks <em>this is me</em> — and the wrong person leaves without wasting your money.</li>
        <li><strong>What transformation do they get?</strong> Nobody buys a tool. They buy the after-state: the version of their business that exists once the problem is gone.</li>
        <li><strong>Why now, and why you?</strong> The mechanism. The reason your way works when everything they've already tried hasn't.</li>
      </ol>
      <p>The rest of this half is just answering those three properly. Question one is your ICP. Question two and three are your offer. Then you pick the funnel shape that carries them, and you write down the arithmetic that says whether it can work.</p>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-the-three-questions-every-funnel-answers"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-1-define-who-you-re-actually-for">Step 1 — Define Who You're Actually For</h3>
      <p>Here's the deal: "small businesses" is not an audience. It's a way of avoiding a decision. A usable definition has two halves — <strong>the account</strong> you're selling to, and <strong>the person inside it</strong> who signs. Most people write the second and skip the first, then wonder why their outreach lists are garbage.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">From a whole market to one winnable account — then to one person</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 512" role="img" aria-label="Ideal customer profile canvas. On the left, five narrowing bands: market, firmographics, stack and spend signals, trigger, and your ICP. On the right, a persona card listing role and seniority, goal, challenge, where they look, buying process, and objection. Along the bottom, a negative-criteria strip listing who to deliberately exclude.">

            <line x1="470" y1="188" x2="826" y2="188" class="gridline"/>
            <text x="470" y="206" class="svlabel" font-size="11.5">Challenge</text><text x="470" y="222" class="svmute" font-size="10">what is stopping them hitting that number</text>
            <line x1="470" y1="236" x2="826" y2="236" class="gridline"/>
            <text x="470" y="254" class="svlabel" font-size="11.5">Where they look</text><text x="470" y="270" class="svmute" font-size="10">publications · communities · who they already follow</text>
            <line x1="470" y1="284" x2="826" y2="284" class="gridline"/>
            <text x="470" y="302" class="svlabel" font-size="11.5">Buying process</text><text x="470" y="318" class="svmute" font-size="10">who else has to sign, and how long it takes</text>
            <line x1="470" y1="332" x2="826" y2="332" class="gridline"/>
            <text x="470" y="350" class="svlabel" font-size="11.5">The objection</text><text x="470" y="366" class="svmute" font-size="10">the one sentence that kills the deal every time</text>
          </g>
          <rect x="16" y="420" width="828" height="74" rx="12" fill="var(--pink-bg)"/>
          <text x="34" y="446" fill="var(--pink)" font-weight="700" font-size="11.5">NEGATIVE CRITERIA · the accounts you deliberately refuse</text>
          <text x="34" y="466" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">too small to afford the outcome · too big to buy without procurement · wrong geography to deliver in</text>
          <text x="34" y="483" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">· no budget owner in the room · a trigger you can't influence. Writing these down is what stops a good list going bad.</text>
        </svg>
        <div class="cap"><b>Both halves or neither.</b> The account definition tells you <b>where to find them</b> — it's what you type into a list-building filter. The persona tells you <b>what to say</b> — it's what your copy is written to.</div>
      </div>
      <h4>The Ten Fields. Fill Every One.</h4>
      <p>Write an actual value in each row. If you can't, that's not a formatting problem — it's the thing you have to go find out.</p>
      <div class="tbl"><table>
        <thead><tr><th>#</th><th>Field</th><th>What "good" looks like</th><th>Where the answer comes from</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td class="num">1</td><td><b>Headcount band</b></td><td>"11–50 employees" — not "SMB"</td><td>Your own closed-won list</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">2</td><td><b>Revenue band</b></td><td>The floor below which they cannot afford the outcome</td><td>Enrichment data · their own site</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">3</td><td><b>Industry</b></td><td>One to three. "All B2B" means you'll write to nobody</td><td>Your best 20 customers</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">4</td><td><b>Geography</b></td><td>Where you can actually deliver and get paid</td><td>Your last 20 deals</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">5</td><td><b>Role &amp; seniority</b></td><td>Owner or decision-maker — someone who can say yes alone</td><td>Who signed, on the deals you won</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">6</td><td><b>Their goal</b></td><td>The number <em>they</em> are measured on, in their words</td><td>Ask ten of them. Not a guess</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">7</td><td><b>Their challenge</b></td><td>What's blocking that number right now</td><td>The same ten</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">8</td><td><b>Where they get information</b></td><td>Named publications, communities, people</td><td>Ask — then go and be there</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">9</td><td><b>Buying process</b></td><td>Who else signs, typical cycle length, usual blocker</td><td>Your <em>lost</em> deals, honestly reviewed</td></tr>
          <tr><td class="num">10</td><td><b>The trigger</b></td><td>The event that makes this urgent this quarter</td><td>Funding · a new hire · a launch · a rule change</td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table></div>
      <div class="srcnote"><b>Fields</b> 1–4 are what you paste into a list-building filter; 5–9 are what your copy is written to; field 10 is the one almost everyone skips and the one that decides your timing.</div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.1</span><b>Find the Pattern in Your Best Customers</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Don't invent an ICP. Extract one. Pull your 20 best accounts — best by <em>revenue, retention and how easy they were to serve</em>, not just revenue — and look for what they share across fields 1–4.</p>
          <ol>
            <li>List the 20 in a sheet, one row each.</li>
            <li>Add a column per firmographic field and fill it from data you already have.</li>
            <li>Find the values that appear in more than half the rows. That's your starting profile.</li>
            <li>Now look at your <em>worst</em> five accounts and write down what <em>they</em> share. That's your first negative criterion.</li>
          </ol>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>Spreadsheet or your CRM</span><span><i>Time</i>45 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>ICP one-pager, fields 1–4</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>Every value traced to a real account, not an opinion</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.2</span><b>Write the Person, and the Objection</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Fields 5–9. If you've spoken to ten customers this year you can write these from memory. If you haven't, <strong>send five messages before tomorrow's session</strong> — one question each: <em>"what number are you personally judged on this quarter, and what's in the way?"</em></p>
          <p>Field 9's answer must include the sentence that kills your deals. Write it verbatim. You'll be answering it on every page you build on Day 2.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>None — or five DMs</span><span><i>Time</i>30 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Persona card, fields 5–9</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>The objection is quoted, not paraphrased</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="fvar">
        <div class="fh">◆ What changes per funnel type · the ICP</div>
        <div class="fb">
          <div class="fc w"><b>Webinar Funnel</b><span>Your ICP has to be <em>list-addressable</em> — you must be able to build a searchable list of them, because you'll be inviting named people to a dated event.</span></div>
          <div class="fc k"><b>Workshop · Challenge</b><span>Add one field: <em>can they show up three days running?</em> A cohort demands repeated attention, which rules out audiences that are structurally too busy.</span></div>
          <div class="fc r"><b>Reverse Squeeze</b><span>Your ICP must be <em>content-reachable</em>. You're leading with value to strangers, so you need a place they already gather — a feed, a community, a search term.</span></div>
        </div>
        <div class="fo"><b>Other types:</b> a book funnel needs an audience that still reads long-form · free-plus-shipping needs a shipping address you can serve · a JV funnel doesn't need an ICP you can reach at all — it needs a <b>partner</b> who already reaches them.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-step-1-define-who-you-re-actually-for"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-2-design-the-offer">Step 2 — Design the Offer</h3>
      <p>An offer is not a price list. It's an argument: <em>this outcome, by this method, in this time, and here's who carries the risk if it doesn't work.</em> There's a clean way to pressure-test that argument before you spend a rupee or a dollar on traffic.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Pressure-test the value, then stack the offer</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 2 860 529" role="img" aria-label="Offer architecture. Top: the value equation shown as a fraction — dream outcome times perceived likelihood, over time delay times effort and sacrifice — with four levers on the right showing which direction to push each. Bottom: five stacked components — transformation, mechanism, proof, guarantee, bonuses — assembling into a single offer statement with a three-tier price strip.">
          <defs>
            <marker id="oa" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="8" markerHeight="8" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path d="M1 1L8 5L1 9" fill="none" stroke="var(--accent)" stroke-width="1.8" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"/></marker>
          </defs>
          <text x="20" y="24" class="svsub" font-size="11">FIRST · IS THE VALUE THERE AT ALL?</text>
          <text x="26" y="128" class="svlabel" font-size="17">VALUE</text>
          <text x="98" y="128" class="svmute" font-size="19">=</text>
          <rect x="128" y="50" width="400" height="58" rx="12" fill="var(--teal-bg)"/>
          <text x="216" y="72" text-anchor="middle" fill="var(--teal)" font-weight="700" font-size="12">Dream outcome</text>
          <text x="216" y="90" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="10">"what exactly do I get?"</text>
          <text x="328" y="80" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="13">×</text>
          <text x="440" y="72" text-anchor="middle" fill="var(--teal)" font-weight="700" font-size="12">Perceived likelihood</text>
          <text x="440" y="90" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="10">"will it work for me?"</text>
          <line x1="128" y1="124" x2="528" y2="124" stroke="var(--ink-2)" stroke-width="2.5"/>
          <rect x="128" y="140" width="400" height="58" rx="12" fill="var(--pink-bg)"/>
          <text x="216" y="162" text-anchor="middle" fill="var(--pink)" font-weight="700" font-size="12">Time delay</text>
          <text x="216" y="180" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="10">"how long until it works?"</text>
          <text x="328" y="170" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="13">×</text>
          <text x="440" y="162" text-anchor="middle" fill="var(--pink)" font-weight="700" font-size="12">Effort &amp; sacrifice</text>
          <text x="440" y="180" text-anchor="middle" class="svmute" font-size="10">"what do I have to do?"</text>
          <g font-size="10.5">
            <rect x="556" y="48" width="288" height="38" rx="9" class="node" stroke="var(--teal)"/>
            <text x="570" y="64" fill="var(--teal)" font-weight="700" font-size="11">↑ Dream outcome</text><text x="570" y="78" class="svmute">make it specific, and make it theirs</text>
            <rect x="556" y="92" width="288" height="38" rx="9" class="node" stroke="var(--teal)"/>
            <text x="570" y="108" fill="var(--teal)" font-weight="700" font-size="11">↑ Perceived likelihood</text><text x="570" y="122" class="svmute">proof, detail, and a real guarantee</text>
            <rect x="556" y="136" width="288" height="38" rx="9" class="node" stroke="var(--pink)"/>
            <text x="570" y="152" fill="var(--pink)" font-weight="700" font-size="11">↓ Time delay</text><text x="570" y="166" class="svmute">engineer a win in the first week</text>
            <rect x="556" y="180" width="288" height="38" rx="9" class="node" stroke="var(--pink)"/>
            <text x="570" y="196" fill="var(--pink)" font-weight="700" font-size="11">↓ Effort &amp; sacrifice</text><text x="570" y="210" class="svmute">do more of the work for them</text>
          </g>
          <text x="20" y="224" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">Beginners push the top — bigger promises, more testimonials. The hard, defensible work is at the bottom: removing time and effort.</text>
          <line x1="20" y1="240" x2="840" y2="240" class="gridline"/>
          <text x="20" y="266" class="svsub" font-size="11">THEN · STACK THE PIECES INTO ONE STATEMENT</text>
          <g font-size="11.5">
            <rect x="24" y="282" width="290" height="42" rx="9" fill="var(--accent-2)"/><text x="40" y="299" fill="var(--accent-ink)" font-weight="700">1 · Transformation</text><text x="40" y="314" class="svmute" font-size="10">the after-state they are actually buying</text>
            <rect x="24" y="330" width="290" height="42" rx="9" fill="var(--accent-2)"/><text x="40" y="347" fill="var(--accent-ink)" font-weight="700">2 · Mechanism</text><text x="40" y="362" class="svmute" font-size="10">why your way works when theirs didn't</text>
            <rect x="24" y="378" width="290" height="42" rx="9" fill="var(--accent-2)"/><text x="40" y="395" fill="var(--accent-ink)" font-weight="700">3 · Proof</text><text x="40" y="410" class="svmute" font-size="10">evidence it has worked before, for someone like them</text>
            <rect x="24" y="426" width="290" height="42" rx="9" fill="var(--accent-2)"/><text x="40" y="443" fill="var(--accent-ink)" font-weight="700">4 · Guarantee</text><text x="40" y="458" class="svmute" font-size="10">who carries the risk — you, or them</text>
            <rect x="24" y="474" width="290" height="42" rx="9" fill="var(--accent-2)"/><text x="40" y="491" fill="var(--accent-ink)" font-weight="700">5 · Bonuses</text><text x="40" y="506" class="svmute" font-size="10">removes the last objection, nothing else</text>
          </g>
          <path d="M324 399 L392 399" fill="none" stroke="var(--accent)" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#oa)" class="flowline"/>
          <rect x="406" y="292" width="438" height="216" rx="14" fill="var(--accent)"/>
          <text x="428" y="320" class="svwhite" font-size="12.5">THE OFFER · one sentence a stranger understands</text>
          <g class="svwhite2" font-size="12.5">
            <text x="428" y="352">I help <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[who]</tspan> go from <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[before]</tspan></text>
            <text x="428" y="376">to <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[after]</tspan> using <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[mechanism]</tspan>,</text>
            <text x="428" y="400">in <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[time]</tspan> — or <tspan font-weight="700" fill="#fff">[guarantee]</tspan>.</text>
          </g>
          <line x1="428" y1="422" x2="822" y2="422" stroke="rgba(255,255,255,.28)" stroke-width="1"/>
          <text x="428" y="444" class="svwhite2" font-size="10.5">and it sells at three points on your ladder:</text>
          <g font-size="10.5">
            <rect x="428" y="458" width="122" height="30" rx="8" fill="rgba(255,255,255,.17)"/><text x="489" y="474" text-anchor="middle" class="svwhite">entry</text>
            <rect x="558" y="458" width="122" height="30" rx="8" fill="rgba(255,255,255,.17)"/><text x="619" y="474" text-anchor="middle" class="svwhite">core</text>
            <rect x="688" y="458" width="134" height="30" rx="8" fill="rgba(255,255,255,.17)"/><text x="755" y="474" text-anchor="middle" class="svwhite">flagship</text>
          </g>
        </svg>
        <div class="cap"><b>Run the equation before you write a word of copy.</b> If the value isn't there, no headline saves it — and if it is, the headline almost writes itself out of the numerator.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="box tip"><div class="h">◆ Pro tip · sell the transformation, not the tool</div>
        <p>"Omnichannel outreach software" is a yawn. "Never wonder where your next ten clients are coming from" is a sale. Same product, completely different funnel. The tool belongs in the <strong>mechanism</strong> line, never the headline.</p></div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.3</span><b>Score Your Offer on All Four Levers</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Four rows, honest scores out of 10, and — this is the part people skip — <strong>one specific change written next to each</strong>.</p>
          <ul>
            <li><strong>Dream outcome</strong> — is it a number and a deadline, or an adjective?</li>
            <li><strong>Perceived likelihood</strong> — what proof does a sceptic see in the first ten seconds?</li>
            <li><strong>Time delay</strong> — what could they achieve in week one? If the answer is "nothing," build a week-one win.</li>
            <li><strong>Effort &amp; sacrifice</strong> — which step could you do <em>for</em> them instead of teaching them to do it?</li>
          </ul>
          <p>Fix your two lowest scores first. Ignore the rest for now.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>None</span><span><i>Time</i>30 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Scored offer + two committed changes</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>Each lever has a concrete change, not a score alone</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.4</span><b>Write the One-Sentence Offer, Then Place It on the Ladder</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Fill the template from the diagram. Then read it out loud to someone who doesn't know your business. If they have to ask a follow-up question, it isn't done.</p>
          <p>Then place it: which rung is this — entry, core, or flagship? And <strong>name the rung above it</strong>, even if you haven't built that yet. A ladder with one rung forces your traffic to be profitable on the first transaction, which is the hardest game there is.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>None</span><span><i>Time</i>20 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Offer sentence + ladder position</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>At least two rungs named and priced</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="fvar">
        <div class="fh">◆ What changes per funnel type · the offer</div>
        <div class="fb">
          <div class="fc w"><b>Webinar Funnel</b><span>The offer arrives once, live, near the end. It has to survive being heard <em>once</em> with no scroll-back — so the guarantee does a lot of work.</span></div>
          <div class="fc k"><b>Workshop · Challenge</b><span>Two offers: the entry price that gets them in, and the ascension offer on the final day. The first one's real job is to <em>qualify</em>, not to profit.</span></div>
          <div class="fc r"><b>Reverse Squeeze</b><span>The offer is never spoken until Part 2. Part 1's only job is to make the mechanism believable — so your mechanism has to be demonstrable, not just describable.</span></div>
        </div>
        <div class="fo"><b>Other types:</b> tripwire and free-plus-shipping funnels put the profit entirely in the upsell, so the front offer can lose money on purpose · a book funnel's offer is the <b>call</b>, not the book · a downsell funnel needs a second offer written <em>before</em> launch, not after the first one underperforms.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-step-2-design-the-offer"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-3-pick-your-funnel-type">Step 3 — Pick Your Funnel Type</h3>
      <p>There's no "best" funnel — only the right one for your offer, your price, and how much your audience already believes. Twenty types, four families. Find the family first; the type inside it is a detail.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Twenty funnel types, grouped by the job they do</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 754" role="img" aria-label="Funnel type taxonomy. Three decision questions across the top, then four family panels: teach to sell, give value first, sell now and ascend later, and let a human or partner close. Each family lists its funnel types with a one-time build-hour estimate. The webinar, workshop or challenge, and reverse squeeze types are marked as the three covered in depth.">

          </g>
          <rect x="438" y="394" width="406" height="244" rx="14" class="node"/>
          <text x="456" y="420" class="svlabel" font-size="12.5">LET A HUMAN OR PARTNER CLOSE</text>
          <text x="456" y="438" class="svmute" font-size="10">a page can't carry the objection — a conversation has to</text>
          <g font-size="11">
            <rect x="454" y="452" width="374" height="34" rx="8" class="node"/><text x="470" y="473" class="svlabel">Book a call</text><text x="812" y="473" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="10">41 h</text>
            <rect x="454" y="493" width="374" height="34" rx="8" class="node"/><text x="470" y="514" class="svlabel">Social following → DM</text><text x="812" y="514" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="10">31 h</text>
            <rect x="454" y="534" width="374" height="34" rx="8" class="node"/><text x="470" y="555" class="svlabel">Final funnel · no sales page</text><text x="812" y="555" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="10">32 h</text>
            <rect x="454" y="575" width="374" height="34" rx="8" class="node"/><text x="470" y="596" class="svlabel">Partnership · JV</text><text x="812" y="596" text-anchor="end" class="svmute" font-size="10">31 h</text>
          </g>
          <text x="456" y="666" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">Highest trust required, lowest volume —</text>
          <text x="456" y="683" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">and the only family where your calendar,</text>
          <text x="456" y="700" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">not your traffic, is the constraint.</text>
          <line x1="16" y1="734" x2="844" y2="734" class="gridline"/>
        </svg>
        <div class="legend2"><span><i style="background:var(--accent)"></i>◆ the three shapes we build in depth — reverse squeeze has two variants</span><span><i style="background:var(--ink-3)"></i>hours = one-time build estimate, not ongoing load</span></div>
        <div class="cap">Every one of the twenty still runs the same six stages from the master map. What the type decides is <b>which stage carries the weight</b> — and therefore where your build hours go.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="box eg"><div class="h">◆ Example · same offer, three different types</div>
        <p>A consultant selling a $999 programme could run any of three. <strong>Webinar</strong> if their audience is list-addressable and needs one idea reframed. <strong>Workshop</strong> if the product <em>is</em> the implementation and they can charge a small entry price to filter. <strong>Reverse squeeze</strong> if nobody's heard of them and the mechanism can be demonstrated in a single piece of content. Same offer. The choice is about <em>belief</em> and <em>reach</em>, not about the product.</p></div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.5</span><b>Choose Your Type — and Write Down Why</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Answer the three questions across the top of the diagram, in order. Then name your type and write <strong>one sentence saying which answer decided it.</strong> That sentence is what you'll check against on Friday when you're tempted to change everything.</p>
          <p>Budget the hours honestly. The estimate next to your type is one-time build, before any traffic. Add the weekly running load: roughly 8–12 h/week for a webinar funnel, 10–15 during a live workshop cohort, 4–8 for a reverse squeeze.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>None</span><span><i>Time</i>15 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Named funnel type + the deciding reason</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>Build hours are in your calendar, not just your notes</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-step-3-pick-your-funnel-type"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-4-write-down-the-arithmetic">Step 4 — Write Down the Arithmetic</h3>
      <p>Last thing today. Before you build anything, you write the equation that says whether this can work — and you write it in <strong>quantities you will actually be able to fetch</strong>, not in conversion rates you made up.</p>
      <p>This is the single biggest difference between a funnel that gets optimised and one that gets abandoned. A rate you invented can never be wrong, so it never teaches you anything. A counted event can be wrong tomorrow, which is exactly what makes it useful.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Every term is a counted event with a named source</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 16 860 433" role="img" aria-label="Funnel math in fetched quantities. A left-to-right rail of eight instrumented steps — visit, opt-in, qualified, engaged, offer seen, checkout started, purchase, renewal — each labelled with the system that reports it, with seven ratio markers between them. Below, a panel of five equations for buyers, revenue, customer acquisition cost, return on ad spend, and lifetime value.">
          <line x1="70" y1="132" x2="784" y2="132" stroke="var(--line-2)" stroke-width="2"/>
          <g fill="var(--accent-2)" stroke="var(--accent)" stroke-width="2">
            <circle cx="70" cy="132" r="12"/><circle cx="172" cy="132" r="12"/><circle cx="274" cy="132" r="12"/><circle cx="376" cy="132" r="12"/>
            <circle cx="478" cy="132" r="12"/><circle cx="580" cy="132" r="12"/>
          </g>
          <g fill="var(--teal-bg)" stroke="var(--teal)" stroke-width="2">
            <circle cx="682" cy="132" r="12"/><circle cx="784" cy="132" r="12"/>
          </g>
          <g font-size="9.5" text-anchor="middle">
            <rect x="106" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="121" y="133" class="svmute">r1</text>
            <rect x="208" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="223" y="133" class="svmute">r2</text>
            <rect x="310" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="325" y="133" class="svmute">r3</text>
            <rect x="412" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="427" y="133" class="svmute">r4</text>
            <rect x="514" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="529" y="133" class="svmute">r5</text>
            <rect x="616" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="631" y="133" class="svmute">r6</text>
            <rect x="718" y="124" width="30" height="17" rx="6" fill="var(--panel)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/><text x="733" y="133" class="svmute">r7</text>
          </g>
          <g text-anchor="middle">
            <text x="70" y="90" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Visit</text><text x="70" y="105" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">analytics</text>
            <text x="274" y="90" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Qualified</text><text x="274" y="105" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">CRM</text>
            <text x="478" y="90" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Offer seen</text><text x="478" y="105" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">analytics</text>
            <text x="682" y="90" class="svlabel" font-size="11" fill="var(--teal)">Purchase</text><text x="682" y="105" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">payment API</text>
            <text x="172" y="168" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Opt-in</text><text x="172" y="183" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">analytics + CRM</text>
            <text x="376" y="168" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Engaged</text><text x="376" y="183" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">event stream</text>
            <text x="580" y="168" class="svlabel" font-size="11">Checkout started</text><text x="580" y="183" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">payment API</text>
            <text x="784" y="168" class="svlabel" font-size="11" fill="var(--teal)">Renewal</text><text x="784" y="183" class="svmute" font-size="9.5">payment + CRM</text>
          </g>
          <text x="20" y="38" class="svsub" font-size="11">THE INSTRUMENTED SPINE · one event per step, one source per event</text>
          <text x="20" y="56" class="svmute" font-size="10">Your stage names will differ by funnel type. The shape — count, count, count — does not.</text>
          <rect x="20" y="212" width="820" height="222" rx="14" fill="var(--panel-2)" stroke="var(--line-2)"/>
          <g style="font-family:ui-monospace,SFMono-Regular,Menlo,Consolas,monospace" font-size="12.5" class="svlabel">
            <text x="42" y="248">Buyers   =  N_visit × r1 × r2 × r3 × r4 × r5 × r6</text>
            <text x="42" y="282">Revenue  =  Buyers × average order value  +  upsell revenue</text>
            <text x="42" y="316">CAC      =  spend on a channel  ÷  buyers from that channel</text>
            <text x="42" y="350">ROAS     =  revenue from a channel  ÷  spend on that channel</text>
            <text x="42" y="384">LTV      =  average order value × (1 + renewals ÷ buyers)</text>
          </g>
          <line x1="42" y1="402" x2="818" y2="402" class="gridline"/>
          <text x="42" y="422" class="svmute" font-size="10.5">Each r is one fetched count divided by another — so a "conversion rate" is a result, never an input. Nothing above is assumed.</text>
        </svg>
        <div class="cap"><b>Today you'll fill this in with holes.</b> That's correct. Anything you can't count yet gets written as <em>unknown — will measure</em>, and Day 3 is where you instrument it so it stops being unknown.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="box do"><div class="h">◆ Do this · the honest baseline</div>
        <p>Write the equation out with your own numbers. For every term you have real data for, <strong>put the number and where it came from.</strong> For every term you don't, write <strong>"unknown — will measure"</strong> and move on. Do not fill a gap with a benchmark you read somewhere. A funnel modelled on borrowed averages will be optimised toward someone else's business.</p></div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 1.6</span><b>Set Your Baseline and Your Target</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>Two lines of arithmetic, working backwards:</p>
          <ol>
            <li><strong>The revenue you want, per month.</strong> A real number.</li>
            <li>Divide by your core offer price → <strong>buyers needed per month.</strong></li>
            <li>Divide by whatever purchase rate you can actually evidence (or mark unknown) → <strong>qualified people needed.</strong></li>
            <li>Keep going up the chain until you reach <strong>visits needed</strong>. That number is your traffic target — and it's the number Day 3's second half is built around.</li>
          </ol>
          <p>If the visits number is absurd, you've learned something valuable on Day 1 rather than in month three: your price, your offer, or your funnel type has to change.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>Calculator below, or a sheet</span><span><i>Time</i>30 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Baseline equation + traffic target</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>Every term is either sourced or explicitly marked unknown</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="fvar">
        <div class="fh">◆ What changes per funnel type · the math</div>
        <div class="fb">
          <div class="fc w"><b>Webinar Funnel</b><span>An extra term almost everyone forgets: <em>show-up rate</em>. Registrations are not attendees, and the gap between them is the single most improvable number in the whole model.</span></div>
          <div class="fc k"><b>Workshop · Challenge</b><span>Two purchase events, not one — entry and ascension. Model them separately or the entry price will look like a failure when it's doing its actual job.</span></div>
          <div class="fc r"><b>Reverse Squeeze</b><span>The leak moves upstream: your r1 isn't opt-in rate, it's <em>consumption depth</em>. Measure how far into Part 1 they get, because that's what predicts everything after it.</span></div>
        </div>
        <div class="fo"><b>Other types:</b> tripwire and free-plus-shipping funnels are modelled on <b>contribution margin, not revenue</b> — the front end is allowed to lose money · a book funnel's real conversion event is the booked call · JV funnels need the revenue-share percentage inside the equation from day one, not bolted on after.</div>
      </div>
      <div class="box do"><div class="h">◆ Your Day 1 output — half one</div>
        <p>Your <strong>funnel strategy on one page</strong>: the ICP and the person inside it, the offer sentence, the ladder it sits on, the funnel type you're building and why, and the baseline arithmetic with its gaps marked. Plus your full 3-day work plan. Every other day is generated from this document — which is why it's worth being uncomfortable about how specific it is.</p></div>
      <div class="box tip"><div class="h">◆ Tonight's homework · 20 minutes</div>
        <p>Send the five ICP questions to five real customers. Tomorrow you turn today's decisions into the <strong>conversion engine</strong> — the single source of context every page, email and ad gets generated from. Thin answers today make a thin engine tomorrow.</p></div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-step-4-write-down-the-arithmetic"></div>
<div class="half" id="d1b">
        <span class="hn">Half two · the conversion engine</span>
        <h2 class="hh" id="d1-turn-those-decisions-into-a-machine-that-writes">Turn Those Decisions Into a Machine That Writes Everything</h2>
        <p class="hl">The first half gave you answers: who, what, which shape. On their own they are a document. This half turns them into <strong>one structured source of truth</strong> that every page, email, ad and outreach message gets generated from — so your funnel tells one story instead of nine.</p>
      </div>
      <h3 id="d1-funnel-vs-campaign-know-the-difference">Funnel vs. Campaign — Know the Difference</h3>
      <div class="grid g2">
        <div class="card"><span class="pill bl">Campaign</span><b style="margin-top:8px">A Moment</b><span>A launch, a promo, a burst. Has a start and an end. Great for spikes, useless for stability.</span></div>
        <div class="card"><span class="pill te">Funnel</span><b style="margin-top:8px">A Machine</b><span>Always on. A stranger can enter today, next week or next year and get the same journey. This is the asset.</span></div>
      </div>
      <p>Campaigns give you a good week. Funnels give you a predictable business. You'll run campaigns <em>into</em> your funnel — but the funnel is what you own.</p>
      <div class="box why"><div class="h">◆ Why this distinction costs people money</div>
        <p>Most businesses rebuild their messaging every campaign. New launch, new deck, new landing page, new emails — all written from scratch, all slightly different. Three campaigns later nobody can say what the company actually promises. <strong>A conversion engine makes that impossible</strong>, because every asset is derived from the same block of context rather than re-invented.</p></div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-funnel-vs-campaign-know-the-difference"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-1-write-the-five-context-blocks">Step 1 — Write the Five Context Blocks</h3>
      <p>Here's the insight most people miss: your landing page, your emails, your ads, your outreach — they all draw from the <strong>same well.</strong> Define it once and every asset stays consistent. Change it once and everything downstream updates.</p>
      <div class="viz">
        <div class="vtitle">Define 5 inputs once → generate every asset</div>

      </div>
      <div class="tbl"><table>
        <thead><tr><th>Block</th><th>What goes in it</th><th>The test that it's done</th></tr></thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><td><b>1 · Offer</b></td><td>The one-sentence offer from Day 1, the price at each rung, the guarantee, what's included, what's explicitly <em>not</em></td><td>A stranger can repeat your offer back correctly</td></tr>
          <tr><td><b>2 · ICP</b></td><td>All ten ICP fields plus the persona card — and the verbatim objection</td><td>You could hand it to a list-builder and get a usable list</td></tr>
          <tr><td><b>3 · Brand</b></td><td>Voice (three adjectives and three anti-adjectives), words you always use, words you never use, colours, type, logo rules</td><td>Two different writers produce copy that sounds like one person</td></tr>
          <tr><td><b>4 · Mechanism</b></td><td>Your named method, the 3–5 steps inside it, and <em>why each step is necessary</em></td><td>It explains why the obvious alternative fails</td></tr>
          <tr><td><b>5 · Proof</b></td><td>Results with numbers, named testimonials, logos, screenshots, third-party data — each tagged to the objection it answers</td><td>Every objection in block 2 has at least one proof against it</td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table></div>
      <div class="box tip"><div class="h">◆ Pro tip · name your mechanism</div>
        <p>An unnamed method is a commodity. A named one is intellectual property. "We do outreach" is a service anyone sells; "The Compounding Invite System — four steps, and here's why step three is the one everyone skips" is a <strong>reason to choose you.</strong> Name it today. You'll use that name on every page you build tomorrow.</p></div>
      <div class="task">
        <div class="th"><span class="tn">Task 2.1</span><b>Fill All Five Blocks in One Document</b></div>
        <div class="tm">
          <p>One file, five headings, no design. Paste yesterday's Day-1 output straight into blocks 1 and 2 — that's what it was for. Then write blocks 3, 4 and 5 fresh.</p>
          <p>Block 5 is where people cheat. Do not write "great results." Write <strong>a number, a name, and a date</strong>. If you have none, say so honestly and use borrowed proof — third-party research, a public case study, your own before-and-after — and label it as borrowed. An empty proof block is a survivable problem. A fake one is not.</p>
        </div>
        <div class="meta"><span><i>Owner</i>You</span><span><i>Tool</i>Any doc, filled live during the session</span><span><i>Time</i>60 min</span><span class="dl"><i>Deliverable</i>Conversion engine document</span><span class="pv"><i>Proof</i>Every objection in block 2 has a matching proof in block 5</span></div>
      </div>
      <div class="vipslot" data-step="d1-step-1-write-the-five-context-blocks"></div>
<h3 id="d1-step-2-build-the-diagnose-then-prescribe-engine">Step 2 — Build the Diagnose-Then-Prescribe Engine</h3>
      <p>Here's the deal: the highest-converting thing you can do to a stranger is not <em>pitch</em> them. It's <strong>diagnose</strong> them — and then prescribe. People don't argue with a conclusion they helped produce.</p>
      <p>This mechanic works everywhere. It's a poll inside a live session, a qualification form on a registration page, an opt-in question on a reverse squeeze bridge, or a full scored assessment. Same engine, different wrapper.</p>
      <div class="viz wide">
        <div class="vtitle">Ask → score → band → prescribe (the reusable engine)</div>
        <svg viewBox="0 0 860 552" role="img" aria-label="Diagnose-then-prescribe engine. Left column: five things you ask, from role and firmographics through agreement statements to goals. Middle column: four score axes that those answers feed, each showing a partial fill. Right column: a nought-to-one-hundred gauge showing sixty-eight, a band label, and a recommended tier card. A footer states that the band-to-tier mapping is a rule stored in the spec, not a judgement call.">
          <defs>
            <marker id="dp" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path d="M1 1L8 5L1 9" fill="none" stroke="var(--accent)" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"/></marker>
            <marker id="dp2" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path d="M1 1L8 5L1 9" fill="none" stroke="var(--teal)" stroke-width="1.8" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"/></marker>
          </defs>
          <text x="16" y="24" class="svsub" font-size="11">1 · WHAT YOU ASK</text>
          <text x="272" y="24" class="svsub" font-size="11">2 · WHAT IT SCORES</text>
          <text x="562" y="24" class="svsub" font-size="11">3 · WHAT THEY GET BACK</text>
